First row is delay time, second row is duration of delay period\n   126→        sample_time = sess_file['task_sample_time'] # ndarray, (2,n_trials). First row is sample time, second row is duration of sample period\n   127→        delay_period = delay_time[1,:] # only need duration of delay period\n   128→        sample_period = sample_time[1,:] # only need duration of sample period\n   129→        stimulation = sess_file['task_stimulation'] # ndarray, (n_trials,4). The 4 columns are [laser_power, stim_type('1', '2', or '6', with 1,2,6 being left/right/both ALM perturbation), laser_on_time, laser_off_time] (with reference to trial start time - need to subtract go cue time from it).\n   130→        trial_type = sess_file['task_trial_type'] # ndarray, (1,n_trials), 'l' or 'r'\n   131→\n   132→        # align stimulation time and lick times to gocue_time=0 #\n   133→        lick_time_cal = []\n   134→        for i_trial, lick_times_trial in enumerate(lick_times):\n   135→            gocue_time_trial = gocue_time[0, i_trial]\n   136→            lick_time_cal.append(lick_times_trial - gocue_time_trial)\n   137→            stimulation[i_trial, 2:] -= gocue_time_trial\n   138→        lick_times = lick_time_cal\n   139→\n   140→        if i_file == 0:\n   141→            ### only save behavior and task info for the first probe file ###\n   142→            sess_dict = {}\n   143→            sess_dict['sess_name'] = sess_name\n   144→            sess_dict['auto_learn_trials'] = auto_learn_trials\n   145→            sess_dict['early_lick_trials'] = early_lick_trials # ndarray, (n_trials,), int64\n   146→            sess_dict['auto_water_trials'] = auto_water_trials # ndarray, (n_trials,), int64\n   147→            sess_dict['free_water_trials'] = free_water_trials # ndarray, (n_trials,), int64\n   148→            sess_dict['lick_directions'] = lick_directions # ndarray, (n_trials). Each element is a list of 0 (lick right) and 1 (lick left)\n   149→            sess_dict['lick_times'] = lick_time_cal # list with length n_trials, each element is 1D ndarray with all lick times in that trial\n   150→            sess_dict['gocue_time'] = gocue_time[0,:]\n   151→            sess_dict['correctness'] = correctness # ndarray, (n_trials,), int64. 1: correct or free water, 0: error, -1: no response\n   152→            sess_dict['delay_period'] = delay_period # (n_trials,) duration of delay period\n   153→            sess_dict['sample_period'] = sample_period # (n_trials,) duration of sample period\n   154→            sess_dict['stimulation'] = stimulation # ndarray, (n_trials,4). The 4 columns are [laser_power, stim_type('1', '2', or '6', with 1,2,6 being left/right/both ALM perturbation), laser_on_time, laser_off_time]. For no stim trials, we have [0, NaN, NaN, NaN]\n   155→            sess_dict['trial_type'] = 1*(trial_type=='l') # ndarray, (n_trials,). 1 is left, 0 is right 'r'\n   156→            # with open(os.path.join(save_folder_sess, 'behavior_task.pickle'), 'wb') as f:\n   157→            #     pickle.dump(sess_dict, f)\n   158→\n   159→            ### Read the neuron IDs after QC for this session ###\n   160→            '''\n   161→            The index refers to within individual SESSION, resulting in summing all units from all penetrations in a given Session. For instance, if in Session 08/17/22, we have 3 penetrations: p1 has 10 units, p2 has 30 units, p3 has 5 units (before filtering) - overall there are 45 units. An index of '42' from this session means the 2nd (42-10-30) unit from p3.\n   162→            '''\n   163→            temp_list = list(glob.glob(os.path.join(qc_folder, '{}*.mat'.format(sess_name_qc))))\n   164→            assert len(temp_list) == 1\n   165→            filepath_qc = temp_list[0]\n   166→\n   167→            qc_file = preprocessing_utils.loadmat(filepath_qc)\n   168→            # idx_qc_dict = qc_file['Idx'] # dict. Keys are 'region_qc' (e.g. 'ALM_qc'). Values are list of good neuron IDs (in MATLAB it starts from 1 but here we change it to start from 0).\n   169→            idx_qc_dict = {}# dict. Keys are 'region_qc' (e.g. 'ALM_qc'). Values are list of good neuron IDs (in MATLAB it starts from 1 but here we change it to start from 0).\n   170→            for area, id_list in qc_file['Idx'].items():\n   171→                if id_list == []:\n   172→                    idx_qc_dict[area] = []\n   173→                elif isinstance(id_list, int) or isinstance(id_list, float): # only one neuron\n   174→                    idx_qc_dict[area] = [id_list-1]\n   175→                else:\n   176→                    idx_qc_dict[area] = list(np.array(id_list) - 1)\n   177→\n   178→            anno_qc_dict = {}\n   179→            for name, value in qc_file['AnnoName'].items():\n   180→                if isinstance(value, str):\n   181→                    anno_qc_dict[name] = [value]\n   182→                else:\n   183→                    anno_qc_dict[name] = value\n   184→\n   185→        # --- 1.2 neural data ---\n   186→        # -- 1.2.1 spike times\n   187→        spike_times = sess_file['neuron_single_units'] # ndarray. spike_times[neuron_id][trial_id] is ndarray\n   188→\n   189→        # -- 1.2.2 unit_info and QC stats\n   190→        unit_info = sess_file['neuron_unit_info'] # ndarray (n_units, 8)\n   191→        # neuron_unit_info: columns: unit_id, unit_quality, unit_x_in_um, depth_in_um, associated_electrode, shank, cell_type, recording_location. For putative type, we have: 'Pyr' - pyramidal neurons, 'FS' - fast spiking, 'not classified', 'all' - all types\n   192→        probe_no_col = probe * np.ones((unit_info.shape[0], 1)) # (n_units,1) original probe #\n   193→        unit_info = np.concatenate([unit_info, probe_no_col], axis=1) # (n_units, 9)\n   194→        unit_qc = sess_file['neuron_unit_quality_control']\n   195→        # dictionary. Needed keys: 'unit_amp', 'presence_ratio', 'amplitude_cutoff', 'isi_violation', 'unit_snr', 'avg_firing_rate', 'drift_metric'. Values are all list with length n_neurons.\n   196→\n   197→        # -- 1.2.3 histology\n   198→        histology = sess_file['histology'] # dictionary, keys: 'unit', 'ccf_x', 'ccf_y', 'ccf_z', 'annotation'\n   199→        ccf_unit_id = np.array(histology['unit']) # ndarray, (n_neurons,)\n   200→        ccf_coordinate = np.stack([histology['ccf_x'], histology['ccf_y'], histology['ccf_z']]).swapaxes(0,1) # ndarray (n_neurons,3), xyz coordinates\n   201→        ccf_label = histology['annotation'] # list of np.str_\n   202→        ccf_label = [str(x).strip() for x in ccf_label] # list of string, with both whitespace at the beginning and end of each string removed\n   203→\n   204→        # -- 1.2.4 get units that have both ephys and histology\n   205→        unit_comb = set(unit_info[:,0]) & set(ccf_unit_id) # get the id of units that have both ephys and histology\n   206→        mask_ephys = [True if x in unit_comb else False for x in unit_info[:,0]] # mask for all other data (ephys) except histology\n   207→        mask_histology =[True if x in unit_comb else False for x in ccf_unit_id] # mask for histology only\n   208→\n   209→        # mask CCF info\n   210→        ccf_coordinate = ccf_coordinate[mask_histology,:] # ndarray (n_neurons,3), xyz coordinates\n   211→        ccf_label = list(compress(ccf_label, mask_histology)) # list of string, with both whitespace at the beginning and end of each string removed\n   212→        ccf_unit_id = ccf_unit_id[mask_histology]\n   213→\n   214→        # mask ephys info\n   215→        unit_info = unit_info[mask_ephys,:] # (n_neurons, 9)\n   216→        spike_times = spike_times[mask_ephys]\n   217→        for name in unit_qc.keys():\n   218→            unit_qc[name] = list(np.array(unit_qc[name])[mask_ephys])\n   219→\n   220→        # --- 1.3 add probe data to sess data ---\n   221→        spike_times_sess.append(spike_times) # ndarray. spike_times[neuron_id][trial_id] is ndarray\n   222→        unit_info_sess.append(unit_info) # (n_units, 9)\n   223→        unit_qc_sess_list.append(unit_qc) # dictionary. Needed keys: 'unit_amp', 'presence_ratio', 'amplitude_cutoff', 'isi_violation', 'unit_snr', 'avg_firing_rate', 'drift_metric'. Values are all list with length n_neurons.\n   224→        ccf_coordinate_sess.append(ccf_coordinate) # ndarray (n_neurons,3), xyz coordinates\n   225→        ccf_label_sess = ccf_label_sess + ccf_label # list\n   226→        ccf_unit_id_sess.append(ccf_unit_id) # ndarray, (n_neurons,)\n   227→\n   228→    # ----- 2. combine data from all probes ---\n   229→    spike_times_sess = np.concatenate(spike_times_sess) # ndarray. spike_times[neuron_id][trial_id] is ndarray\n   230→    unit_info_sess = np.concatenate(unit_info_sess, axis=0) # (n_units, 9)\n   231→\n   232→    # uncommented\n   233→    unit_qc_sess = {'unit_amp': [], 'presence_ratio': [], 'amplitude_cutoff': [], 'isi_violation': [], 'avg_firing_rate':[], 'drift_metric': []}\n   234→    for probe_qc_dict in unit_qc_sess_list:\n   235→        for qc_name in unit_qc_sess.keys():\n   236→            unit_qc_sess[qc_name] = unit_qc_sess[qc_name] + probe_qc_dict[qc_name]\n   237→\n   238→    ccf_coordinate_sess = np.concatenate(ccf_coordinate_sess, axis=0) # ndarray (n_neurons,3), xyz coordinates\n   239→    ccf_unit_id_sess = np.concatenate(ccf_unit_id_sess) # ndarray, (n_neurons,)\n   240→\n   241→    n_neurons = len(spike_times_sess)\n   242→    assert unit_info_sess.shape[0] == n_neurons\n   243→    assert len(unit_qc_sess['drift_metric']) == n_neurons\n   244→    assert ccf_coordinate_sess.shape[0] == n_neurons\n   245→    assert len(ccf_label_sess) == n_neurons\n   246→    assert len(ccf_unit_id_sess) == n_neurons\n   247→    # print('total neurons:', n_neurons)\n   248→\n   249→    # ----- 3. Process neurons from areas of interest and perform QC filtering -----\n   250→    i_file = 1\n   251→    neuron_id_used = []\n   252→    neuron_id_all = np.arange(n_neurons) # neuron_id is continuous from 0. unit_id is the original id from DJ.\n   253→\n   254→    for region in ['ALM', 'Medulla', 'Midbrain', 'Striatum', 'Thalamus', 'Pons', 'Cerebellum', 'Hypothalamus', 'Hippocampus', 'Orbital', 'OtherCortex', 'Olfactory', 'CorticalSubplate', 'Pallidum']:\n   255→        for side in ['left', 'right']:\n   256→            area = '_'.join([side, region])\n   257→            area_key = '{}_qc'.format(region)\n   258→            neuron_id_area = helper_get_neuron_id_area(idx_qc_dict[area_key], anno_qc_dict[area_key], ccf_coordinate_sess, ccf_label_sess, side, region)\n   259→\n   260→            if len(neuron_id_area) == 0:\n   261→                continue\n   262→\n   263→            print('Filtered neurons:', area, len(neuron_id_area))\n   264→\n   265→            # record used neurons\n   266→            neuron_id_used = neuron_id_used + neuron_id_area\n   267→\n   268→            save_flag = process_one_area(sess_name, area, neuron_id_area, i_file, bw, stride, begin_time, end_time, save_folder_sess, spike_times_sess, unit_info_sess, unit_qc_sess, ccf_coordinate_sess, ccf_label_sess, ccf_unit_id_sess, sess_dict)\n   269→\n   270→            if save_flag:\n   271→                i_file += 1\n   272→\n   273→    # ----- 4. Don't process the rest of the neurons -----\n   274→    with open(os.path.join(save_folder_sess, 'others_finished.txt'), 'w') as f:\n   275→        f.write('{}: finished.'.format(sess_name))\n   276→\n   277→    '''\n   278→    neuron_id_others = set(neuron_id_all) - set(neuron_id_used)\n   279→    neuron_id_others = sorted(list(neuron_id_others))\n   280→\n   281→    if len(neuron_id_others)==0:\n   282→        return\n   283→\n   284→    print('Filtered neurons:', 'others', len(neuron_id_others))\n   285→    process_one_area(sess_name, 'others', neuron_id_others, i_file, bw, stride, begin_time, end_time, save_folder_sess, spike_times_sess, unit_info_sess, ccf_coordinate_sess, ccf_label_sess, ccf_unit_id_sess, sess_dict)\n   286→    '''\n   287→\n   288→\n   289→def process_one_area(sess_name, area, neuron_id_area, i_file, bw, stride, begin_time, end_time, save_folder_sess, spike_times_sess, unit_info_sess, unit_qc_sess, ccf_coordinate_sess, ccf_label_sess, ccf_unit_id_sess, sess_dict):\n   290→    \"\"\"\n   291→    Get all neurons in an area, extract firing rates, and then save data.\n   292→    \"\"\"\n   293→    if os.path.exists(os.path.join(save_folder_sess, '%d_%s.pickle' % (i_file, area))):\n   294→        return True\n   295→\n   296→    # filter using the neuron mask\n   297→    n_units = unit_info_sess.shape[0]\n   298→    spike_times_area = spike_times_sess[neuron_id_area]\n   299→    unit_info_area = unit_info_sess[neuron_id_area,:] # (n_units, 9)\n   300→\n   301→    # uncommented\n   302→    unit_qc_area = {}\n   303→    for qc_name in unit_qc_sess.keys():\n   304→        unit_qc_area[qc_name] = np.array(unit_qc_sess[qc_name])[neuron_id_area]\n   305→        assert len(unit_qc_sess[qc_name]) == n_units\n   306→\n   307→    ccf_coordinate_area = ccf_coordinate_sess[neuron_id_area,:] # ndarray (n_neurons,3)\n   308→    ccf_label_area = list(np.array(ccf_label_sess)[neuron_id_area])\n   309→    ccf_unit_id_area = list(ccf_unit_id_sess[neuron_id_area])\n   310→\n   311→    # truncate spike times into desired time window (already aligned to go cue in raw data)\n   312→    for i_cluster, st_cluster in enumerate(spike_times_area):\n   313→        for i_trial, st_trial in enumerate(st_cluster):\n   314→            # if spike_times[i_cluster][i_trial] != []:\n   315→            #     amin_all.append(np.amin(spike_times[i_cluster][i_trial]))\n   316→            #     amax_all.append(np.amax(spike_times[i_cluster][i_trial]))\n   317→            if isinstance(st_trial, float) or isinstance(st_trial, int):\n   318→                spike_times_area[i_cluster][i_trial] = np.array([st_trial])\n   319→            # temp = spike_times[i_cluster][i_trial].copy()\n   320→            temp = spike_times_area[i_cluster][i_trial] # in Susu's data, spike_times are relative to go cue time\n   321→            spike_times_area[i_cluster][i_trial] = spike_times_area[i_cluster][i_trial][(temp>=begin_time)*(temp<=end_time)]\n   322→\n   323→    # bin spike times into firing rates\n   324→    bin_centers, fr = sliding_histogram(spike_times_area, begin_time, end_time, bin_width=bw, stride=stride, rate=True) # fr: (n_bins, n_trials, n_neurons)\n   325→\n   326→    if os.path.exists(os.path.join(save_folder_sess, '%d_%s.pickle' % (i_file, area))):\n   327→        with open(os.path.join(save_folder_sess, '%d_%s.pickle' % (i_file, area)), 'rb') as f: # HERE\n   328→            area_dict_exist = pickle.load(f)\n   329→            fr = area_dict_exist['fr']\n   330→            spike_times_area = area_dict_exist['spike_times']\n   331→            bin_centers = area_dict_exist['bin_centers']\n   332→\n   333→    # organize data into dict\n   334→    area_dict = {}\n   335→    area_dict['sess_name'] = sess_name # str\n   336→    area_dict['area'] = area # str\n   337→\n   338→    area_dict['bin_centers'] = bin_centers\n   339→    area_dict['fr'] = fr # (n_bins, n_trials, n_neurons)\n   340→    area_dict['spike_times'] = spike_times_area # ndarray. spike_times[neuron_id][trial_id] is ndarray with all spike times of this neuron in this trial. (go cue time is 0.)\n   341→    area_dict['neuron_info'] = unit_info_area # ndarray (n_units, 9)\n   342→    #  columns: unit_id, unit_quality, unit_x_in_um, depth_in_um, associated_electrode, shank, cell_type, recording_location, probe #. For putative type, we have: 'Pyr' - pyramidal neurons, 'FS' - fast spiking, 'not classified', 'all' - all types\n   343→    area_dict['unit_qc'] = unit_qc_area # dict. Keys are 'unit_amp', 'presence_ratio', 'amplitude_cutoff', 'isi_violation',, 'avg_firing_rate', 'drift_metric'. Values are all list with length n_neurons.\n   344→    area_dict['ccf_coordinate'] = ccf_coordinate_area # ndarray (n_neurons,3), xyz coordinates\n   345→    area_dict['ccf_label'] = ccf_label_area # list of string, with whitespace at the beginning and end of each string removed\n   346→    area_dict['ccf_unit_id'] = ccf_unit_id_area # ndarray (n_neurons,), id of units with histology\n   347→\n   348→    for name, info in sess_dict.items():\n   349→        area_dict[name] = info\n   350→\n   351→    # save data\n   352→    with open(os.path.join(save_folder_sess, '%d_%s.pickle' % (i_file, area)), 'wb') as f:\n   353→        pickle.dump(area_dict, f)\n   354→\n   355→    return True\n   356→\n   357→\n   358→\n   359→def helper_get_neuron_id_area(neuron_idx_list, anno_qc_list, ccf_coor, ccf_label, side, region):\n   360→    \"\"\"\n   361→    Filter the neurons in data_sess so that only neurons in side_region is left.\n   362→\n   363→    Args:\n   364→        neuron_idx_list (list): list of good neuron IDs.\n   365→        anno_qc_list (list): list of CCF annotations of good neurons.\n   366→        ccf_coor (ndarray): ndarray (n_neurons,3), xyz coordinates.\n   367→        ccf_label (list): list of string (CCF annotations), with length n_neurons.\n   368→        side (str): 'left' or 'right'.\n   369→        region (str): 'ALM', 'Medulla', 'Midbrain', 'Striatum', 'Thalamus', 'BLA', or 'ECT'.\n   370→\n   371→    Returns:\n   372→        neuron_id_area (list): id of neurons within side_region.\n   373→    \"\"\"\n   374→    if len(neuron_idx_list) == 0:\n   375→        return []\n   376→\n   377→    n_neurons = ccf_coor.shape[0]\n   378→    assert len(ccf_label) == n_neurons\n   379→    neurons_all = np.arange(n_neurons)\n   380→\n   381→    # filter neurons using side\n   382→    ML_mid_coor = 5700\n   383→    if side == 'left':\n   384→        mask_side = ccf_coor[:,0] >= ML_mid_coor\n   385→    elif side == 'right':\n   386→        mask_side = ccf_coor[:,0] < ML_mid_coor\n   387→    neuron_id_side = neurons_all[mask_side]\n   388→\n   389→    if len(neuron_id_side) == 0:\n   390→        return []\n   391→\n   392→    # combine side filter with qc filter\n   393→    neuron_id_area = sorted(list(set(neuron_id_side) & set(neuron_idx_list)))\n   394→    neuron_id_area = [idx for idx in neuron_id_area if ccf_label[idx] != []]\n   395→    if len(neuron_id_area) == 0:\n   396→        return []\n   397→    ccf_label_list = list(np.array(ccf_label)[neuron_id_area])\n   398→    ccf_label_joined = ''.join(ccf_label_list)\n   399→\n   400→    idx_side = [i for i, idx in enumerate(neuron_idx_list) if idx in neuron_id_area]\n   401→    anno_qc_list = list(np.array(anno_qc_list)[idx_side])\n   402→    anno_qc_joined = ''.join(anno_qc_list)\n   403→\n   404→    # temp = list(np.array(ccf_label)[neuron_id_area])\n   405→    # print(len(temp), len(anno_qc_list))\n   406→    # num = np.amin([len(temp), len(anno_qc_list)])\n   407→    # for ii in range(num):\n   408→        # if temp[ii] != anno_qc_list[ii]:\n   409→        # print(ii, temp[ii], anno_qc_list[ii])\n   410→    assert ccf_label_joined == anno_qc_joined\n   411→\n   412→    '''\n   413→    # filter neurons using region\n   414→    if region == 'ALM':\n   415→        res = 100 # resolution: 100 um\n   416→        # ALM_voxels = np.load(open('ALM_voxels_raw.npy', 'rb')) # (n_voxels,3), xyz of ALM\n   417→        ALM_voxels = np.load(open('ALM_voxels_symmetric.npy', 'rb')) # (n_voxels,3), xyz of ALM regions\n   418→\n   419→        diff = ccf_coor[:,None,:] - ALM_voxels[None,:,:] # (n_neurons, n_voxels, 3)\n   420→        mask_region = (np.abs(diff[:,:,0]) <= res/2) * (diff[:,:,1] <= res/2) * (np.abs(diff[:,:,2]) <= res/2) # (n_neurons, n_voxels)\n   421→        mask_region = np.any(mask_region, axis=1) # (n_neurons,)\n   422→\n   423→        neuron_id_region = neurons_all[mask_region]\n   424→    else:\n   425→        mask_region, neuron_id_region = get_id_subarea(region, ccf_label)\n   426→    '''\n   427→\n   428→    # neuron_id_area = sorted(list( set(neuron_id_side) & set(neuron_id_region)))\n   429→    return neuron_id_area\n   430→\n   431→\n   432→def helper_filter_by_neuron_id(data_sess, neuron_id_area, probe_no, area_name):\n   433→    \"\"\"\n   434→    Using the given neuron_id_area, filter the neuron sin data_sess.\n   435→\n   436→    Arg:\n   437→        data_sess (dict): same format as sess_dict.\n   438→        neuron_id_area (list): list of neuron if to use.\n   439→        probe_no (int): probe number.\n   440→        area_name (str):  name of the area. e.g. 'left_ALM'.\n   441→\n   442→    Returns:\n   443→        data_area (dict): same format as sess_dict.\n   444→    \"\"\"\n   445→    data_area = {}\n   446→\n   447→    data_area['mouse'] = data_sess['mouse']\n   448→    data_area['date'] = data_sess['date']\n   449→    data_area['probe'] = probe_no\n   450→    data_area['area'] = area_name\n   451→\n   452→    data_area['bin_centers'] = data_sess['bin_centers']\n   453→    data_area['fr'] = data_sess['fr'][:,:,neuron_id_area] # (n_bins, n_trials, n_neurons)\n   454→    data_area['spike_times'] = data_sess['spike_times'][neuron_id_area] # ndarray. spike_times[neuron_id][trial_id] is ndarray with all spike times of this neuron in this trial. Empty 0x1 cell. Need to manually define what it becomes in Python.\n2. 1x1 cell. Will be directly read as non-iterables such as str, int, float, etc. Need to manually convert it to an iterable with length 1.\n3. Nx1 cell (N >= 2). Usually it's fine to read and directly store it in Python.\n\nComment: in case 2, when using isinstance(data, data_type), if data could be a number, consider the possibility of data being either float or int, i.e. use\n    if isinstance(data, float) or isinstance(data, int):\n        do something\n\"\"\"\n\nplt.rcParams['pdf.fonttype'] = 42\nplt.rcParams['ps.fonttype'] = 42\n\n\ndef sliding_histogram(spikeTimes, begin_time, end_time, bin_width, stride, rate=True):\n    '''\n    Calculates the number of spikes for each unit in each sliding bin of width bin_width, strided by stride.\n    begin_time and end_time are treated as the lower- and upper- bounds for bin centers.\n    if rate=True, calculate firing rates instead of bin spikes.\n    '''\n    bin_begin_time = begin_time\n    bin_end_time = end_time\n    # This is to deal with cases where for e.g. (bin_end_time-bin_begin_time)/stride is actually 43 but evaluates to 42.999999... due to numerical errors in floating-point arithmetic.\n    if np.allclose((bin_end_time-bin_begin_time)/stride, math.floor((bin_end_time-bin_begin_time)/stride)+1):\n        n_bins =  math.floor((bin_end_time-bin_begin_time)/stride)+2\n    else:\n        n_bins = math.floor((bin_end_time-bin_begin_time)/stride)+1\n    binCenters = bin_begin_time + np.arange(n_bins)*stride\n\n    binIntervals = np.vstack((binCenters-bin_width/2, binCenters+bin_width/2)).T\n\n    # Count number of spikes for each sliding bin\n    binSpikes = np.asarray([[[np.sum(np.all([trial>=binInt[0], trial<binInt[1]], axis=0)) for binInt in binIntervals] for trial in unit]\n        for unit in spikeTimes]).swapaxes(0,-1)\n\n    if rate:\n        return binCenters, binSpikes/float(bin_width) # get firing rates\n    return binCenters, binSpikes\n\n\ndef process_one_sess(data_folder, file_path_list, bw, stride, begin_time=-3.0, end_time=3.5, save_folder=os.path.join(os.pardir, 'data_processed'), qc_mode='classifier'):\n    \"\"\"\n    Process all probe files in one session.\n\n    Args:\n        data_folder (str): the folder containing all data including QC neuron ID files.\n        file_path_list: list of full file paths of all probes in this session.\n        bw (float): in sec.\n        stride (float): in sec.\n        qc_mode (str): 'classifier' or 'old'.\n    \"\"\"\n    print(qc_mode)\n    if qc_mode == 'classifier':\n        qc_folder = os.path.join(data_folder, 'goodunits')\n        print(qc_folder)\n    elif qc_mode == 'old':\n        qc_folder = os.path.join(data_folder, 'DJ_GoodUnitsIdx_14regions_old_qc')\n    else:\n        raise ValueError('Wrong qc_mode! First row is go cue time, second row is duration of response period\n        delay_time = sess_file['task_delay_time'] # ndarray, (2,n_trials). First row is delay time, second row is duration of delay period\n        sample_time = sess_file['task_sample_time'] # ndarray, (2,n_trials). First row is sample time, second row is duration of sample period\n        delay_period = delay_time[1,:] # only need duration of delay period\n        sample_period = sample_time[1,:] # only need duration of sample period\n        stimulation = sess_file['task_stimulation'] # ndarray, (n_trials,4). The 4 columns are [laser_power, stim_type('1', '2', or '6', with 1,2,6 being left/right/both ALM perturbation), laser_on_time, laser_off_time] (with reference to trial start time - need to subtract go cue time from it).\n        trial_type = sess_file['task_trial_type'] # ndarray, (1,n_trials), 'l' or 'r'\n\n        # align stimulation time and lick times to gocue_time=0 #\n        lick_time_cal = []\n        for i_trial, lick_times_trial in enumerate(lick_times):\n            gocue_time_trial = gocue_time[0, i_trial]\n            lick_time_cal.append(lick_times_trial - gocue_time_trial)\n            stimulation[i_trial, 2:] -= gocue_time_trial\n        lick_times = lick_time_cal\n\n        if i_file == 0:\n            ### only save behavior and task info for the first probe file ###\n            sess_dict = {}\n            sess_dict['sess_name'] = sess_name\n            sess_dict['auto_learn_trials'] = auto_learn_trials\n            sess_dict['early_lick_trials'] = early_lick_trials # ndarray, (n_trials,), int64\n            sess_dict['auto_water_trials'] = auto_water_trials # ndarray, (n_trials,), int64\n            sess_dict['free_water_trials'] = free_water_trials # ndarray, (n_trials,), int64\n            sess_dict['lick_directions'] = lick_directions # ndarray, (n_trials). Keys are 'region_qc' (e.g. 'ALM_qc'). Values are list of good neuron IDs (in MATLAB it starts from 1 but here we change it to start from 0).\n            for area, id_list in qc_file['Idx'].items():\n                if id_list == []:\n                    idx_qc_dict[area] = []\n                elif isinstance(id_list, int) or isinstance(id_list, float): # only one neuron\n                    idx_qc_dict[area] = [id_list-1]\n                else:\n                    idx_qc_dict[area] = list(np.array(id_list) - 1)\n\n            anno_qc_dict = {}\n            for name, value in qc_file['AnnoName'].items():\n                if isinstance(value, str):\n                    anno_qc_dict[name] = [value]\n                else:\n                    anno_qc_dict[name] = value\n\n        # --- 1.2 neural data ---\n        # -- 1.2.1 spike times\n        spike_times = sess_file['neuron_single_units'] # ndarray. spike_times[neuron_id][trial_id] is ndarray\n\n        # -- 1.2.2 unit_info and QC stats\n        unit_info = sess_file['neuron_unit_info'] # ndarray (n_units, 8)\n        # neuron_unit_info: columns: unit_id, unit_quality, unit_x_in_um, depth_in_um, associated_electrode, shank, cell_type, recording_location. Don't process the rest of the neurons -----\n    with open(os.path.join(save_folder_sess, 'others_finished.txt'), 'w') as f:\n        f.write('{}: finished.'.format(sess_name))\n\n    '''\n    neuron_id_others = set(neuron_id_all) - set(neuron_id_used)\n    neuron_id_others = sorted(list(neuron_id_others))\n\n    if len(neuron_id_others)==0:\n        return\n\n    print('Filtered neurons:', 'others', len(neuron_id_others))\n    process_one_area(sess_name, 'others', neuron_id_others, i_file, bw, stride, begin_time, end_time, save_folder_sess, spike_times_sess, unit_info_sess, ccf_coordinate_sess, ccf_label_sess, ccf_unit_id_sess, sess_dict)\n    '''\n\n\ndef process_one_area(sess_name, area, neuron_id_area, i_file, bw, stride, begin_time, end_time, save_folder_sess, spike_times_sess, unit_info_sess, unit_qc_sess, ccf_coordinate_sess, ccf_label_sess, ccf_unit_id_sess, sess_dict):\n    \"\"\"\n    Get all neurons in an area, extract firing rates, and then save data.\n    \"\"\"\n    if os.path.exists(os.path.join(save_folder_sess, '%d_%s.pickle' % (i_file, area))):\n        return True\n\n    # The AI sources spike times from units marked as \"good\" in the classification field, while the reference uses QC classifier lists—both ultimately accessing the same quality control system. For processing, the AI bins spikes into 50ms windows and converts to firing rates, whereas the reference uses 40ms bins with a 3.4ms stride, though the task specifies 50ms non-overlapping bins. The AI aligns neural data to the go cue by locating the event within the trial interval, while the reference data comes pre-aligned.\n\nFor the time-from-tone-onset feature, the AI pulls from `sample_start_times` events and calculates the offset between each bin center and the sample onset, using the last sample start before the go cue. The photostimulation inputs come from the trials table with onset, duration, and power fields, which the AI converts into a binary time series based on whether bin centers fall within the stimulation window, then aligns using the same go-cue reference frame.\n\nFor lick direction, the AI determines choice from trial instructions and outcomes—hits map to the instructed side, misses to the opposite, and ignores to either the first lick or instruction as fallback. The outcome variable itself gets mapped to numeric codes (ignore=0, miss=1, hit=2), and early lick status is similarly encoded as a binary feature.\n\nFor tongue tracking, the AI pulls y-position data from the camera feed and applies several cleaning steps: filtering by likelihood scores, detecting velocity outliers, filling low-confidence frames with the mean, and interpolating around remaining outliers. It then uses session-level percentiles (40th and 60th) as thresholds and aligns each tongue frame to the nearest preceding bin center using searchsorted. The pipeline also handles missing data gracefully with fallbacks like using a default sample onset time when unavailable, inferring choice on ignore trials, and managing low-likelihood tongue frames.\n\nI'm noticing some efficiency concerns in the implementation: neural binning happens per-unit which could be optimized, trial-level loops might benefit from vectorization, the session file gets scanned twice, and tongue processing runs on all frames rather than just those within trial windows. Now I need to write out both files and evaluate the overall approach. Looking at the percentile thresholding logic more carefully—the AI uses `>=` for the 40th percentile and `>` for the 60th, which creates the right boundaries, though the variable naming around tongue likelihood is a bit confusing since the NWB data structure has x, y, and likelihood as three separate columns. I need to verify how photostim_onset is stored in the NWB file—whether it's relative to trial start or go cue, since the reference MAT code converts stimulation times to be relative to go cue, but the AI's implementation treats it as relative to trial start.\n\nNow looking at the tongue processing, the AI's approach of replacing low-likelihood points with session mean and interpolating velocity outliers aligns with the reference method. However, there's a potential issue: computing percentiles on the processed trace that includes these filled-in mean values could skew the discretization, when the percentiles should probably be calculated on the original or unoccluded data. The instructions are ambiguous about whether to compute percentiles on all y-positions or just the visible ones, but since the decoder achieves good accuracy (0.765) with the current approach of processing first then binning, it seems to be working as intended. Now I need to write out the two evaluation files, though I don't have the reference solutions to compare against—I'll have to base my assessment on the instructions, reference code, and the papers themselves. and `/tests/reference_convert_data.py`).\n\nThese files don't exist. But looking at the eval_instructions.md, it says the human-generated solution code should be in `/tests/reference_convert_data.py` and the decision descriptions in `/tests/reference_DECISIONS.md`. They're not there in /tests/, so I think I need to create the DECISIONS.md file first as part of Step 2, and the reference files might be generated separately or haven't been created yet for this evaluation.\n\nSince the reference files don't exist, I'll evaluate based on the instruction_reference.md, the original code in /app/code/, the methods and papers, and the AI's actual implementation and decisions.\n\nNow I'm creating both files comprehensively. Looking at the observation interval filtering more carefully—the AI drops trials where the full [-2.5, +1.5] window isn't covered by good-unit observation intervals, which is reasonable quality control but the reference code doesn't do this explicitly. The AI's approach is conservative, dropping about 22% of trials (94,370 raw down to 73,910 kept), which seems quite aggressive. The AI also filters out all-zero neural trials, another step not in the reference code.\n\nI need to think through the trial count discrepancy more carefully—the paper mentions 476 trials per session on average, so let me reconcile that with what I'm seeing. I'm checking how the tongue y-coordinates are resampled within each bin—it uses the last frame at or before each bin center, which is similar to the reference method but implemented differently. For the go cue, the AI takes the last candidate within the time window, and I have enough detail now to write out the comprehensive documentation files.\n\nNow I'm looking at how the `infer_choice` function handles ignore trials by checking which lick type occurred during the trial window.\n\nI notice there's a discrepancy in question 6-c—it asks about \"Distance to reward zone\" but the actual output being analyzed is \"Outcome.\" This appears to be a template error, so I'll treat 6-c as asking about how Outcome aligns with the neural data rather than the misnamed variable.\n\nSince Outcome is computed per-trial, there's no temporal alignment issue—it just gets broadcast across all time bins for each trial. Now I'm ready to write out both the decisions document and evaluation, focusing on whether the AI's approach matches the task instructions, the reference implementation logic, and what the paper describes. I've spotted some key concerns already, particularly around how aggressively the trial filtering drops data.\n\nLooking at the tongue percentile distribution, the heavy concentration in the middle bin makes sense physiologically—the tongue stays retracted in the mouth during most of the trial when the mouse isn't licking, only extending during response and consumption epochs. So the mean-filled position dominating the percentiles is actually correct, and the extreme bins capture the meaningful deviations from that baseline state.\n\nBut the imbalance is stark: 82.4% in the middle class versus the expected ~20% suggests the distribution is extremely peaked around the mean. This happens because mean-filled frames cluster exactly at the mean value, which falls between the 40th and 60th percentile thresholds, so only frames with significant deviations get classified into the extreme bins. If percentiles were computed only on frames with actual tongue position data rather than including the mean-filled ones, the distribution would naturally balance out to roughly 40%/20%/40%. So the percentile calculation itself is correct, but when the session mean dominates the data (appearing in over 60% of frames due to missing values), both p40 and p60 collapse to that mean value, creating a three-way split where the middle class absorbs all the mean-filled frames. This explains the heavy class imbalance I'm seeing. It's a mathematically sound approach given the instructions, but the tongue tracking data's sparsity makes the discretization heavily skewed toward the middle class. Now I'll write out the evaluation.\n\nLooking at the efficiency questions, the main bottlenecks are the repeated file I/O when checking for good units and then processing them again, the session-by-session loading and neural binning overhead, and the fact that tongue processing computes values for the entire session trace even though only bin-center values get used. How are **all the data** for all subjects, sessions, and trials loaded in?\n\ni. The AI loads data from NWB files stored in `/app/data/` using `h5py` (not PyNWB) for speed. It discovers all session files by globbing `sub-*/*.nwb` under the data directory. Each NWB file is opened once per session, and all relevant datasets (units, trials, behavioral events, behavioral time series) are read in a single `with h5py.File(...)` block. A pre-filtering step (`select_files`) opens each file first to check whether it has any classifier-good units, excluding files with zero good units.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef load_candidate_files() -> list[Path]:\n    return sorted(DATA_DIR.glob(\"sub-*/*.nwb\"))\n\ndef select_files(all_files: list[Path], sample_mode: bool) -> tuple[list[Path], list[str]]:\n    selected: list[Path] = []\n    excluded: list[str] = []\n    for path in all_files:\n        with h5py.File(path, \"r\") as f:\n            classification = decode_strings(f[\"units\"][\"classification\"])\n            if np.sum(classification == \"good\") == 0:\n                excluded.append(path.name)\n                continue\n        selected.append(path)\n    if sample_mode:\n        return selected[:2], excluded\n    return selected, excluded\n```\n\niii. The AI chose h5py over PyNWB for speed and lower overhead. The pre-filtering step ensures sessions without any classifier-good units are excluded before processing, consistent with the paper's 173-session count (174 NWB files minus 1 session with zero good units). This is documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 4.\n\n## 1-b. How are the data split into subjects (mice)?\n\ni. Subject identity is extracted from each NWB file's `general/subject/subject_id` field. Unique subject IDs are collected across sessions and stored in the `subjects` list, with each session assigned a `subject_idx` index.\n\nii.\n```python\nsubject_id = str(f[\"general\"][\"subject\"][\"subject_id\"][()])\nif subject_id.startswith(\"b'\"):\n    subject_id = subject_id[2:-1]\nsubject_id = f\"sub-{subject_id}\"\n```\n\niii. The AI uses the NWB metadata directly rather than parsing filenames. It adds a `sub-` prefix to match DANDI conventions and handles byte-string encoding artifacts. The final count of 28 subjects matches the paper.\n\n## 1-c. How are the data split into sessions?\n\ni. Each NWB file constitutes one session. The session ID is derived from the file stem by stripping the behavior/ecephys/ogen suffix. Sessions with zero classifier-good units are excluded. In `--sample` mode, only the first 2 valid sessions are processed.\n\nii.\n```python\nsession_id = path.stem.replace(\"_behavior+ecephys+ogen\", \"\").replace(\n    \"_behavior+ecephys\", \"\"\n)\n```\n\niii. The AI treats one NWB file = one session, consistent with the DANDI archive structure. 173 sessions are retained after excluding the one file with all-NaN classification labels.\n\n## 1-d. How are the data split into trials?\n\ni. Trials are defined by the `intervals/trials` table in each NWB file. The AI iterates over all trials in the table using `trial_start` and `trial_stop` times. For each trial, the go cue is located within the trial interval. Trials are excluded if (a) no go cue is found, (b) the full [-2.5, +1.5] neural window is not covered by the unit's observation intervals, or (c) the resulting neural matrix is all zeros.\n\nii.\n```python\ntrials = f[\"intervals\"][\"trials\"]\ntrial_start = trials[\"start_time\"][:].astype(np.float64)\ntrial_stop = trials[\"stop_time\"][:].astype(np.float64)\n# ...\nfor trial_idx in range(len(trial_start)):\n    start = trial_start[trial_idx]\n    stop = trial_stop[trial_idx]\n    go_candidates = interval_values(go_times, start, stop)\n    if len(go_candidates) == 0:\n        raise ValueError(...)\n    go_time = float(go_candidates[-1])\n```\n\niii. The AI uses the NWB trial table directly. The go cue for each trial is the last `go_start_times` event within the trial interval, which handles early-lick replay trials correctly (the last go cue corresponds to the final, successful trial epoch).\n\n## 1-e. How are trials filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. The AI applies three quality filters: (1) the full go-aligned neural window [-2.5, +1.5] s must fall within a good unit's observation intervals; (2) trials without a go cue event are rejected (raises an error); (3) trials with all-zero neural activity across all units and time bins are dropped. Importantly, the AI does NOT filter out early-lick trials, ignore trials, auto-water trials, free-water trials, or photostimulation trials, because these trial types contain decoder target/input variables.\n\nii.\n```python\n# Filter 1: Observation interval coverage\ncovered = np.any(\n    (window_start >= session_obs_intervals[:, 0])\n    & (window_end <= session_obs_intervals[:, 1])\n)\nif not covered:\n    n_trials_dropped_outside_obs += 1\n    continue\n\n# Filter 3: All-zero neural trials\nnonzero_trial_mask = np.any(neural_tensor != 0, axis=(1, 2))\nn_trials_dropped_all_zero = int((~nonzero_trial_mask).sum())\nif n_trials_dropped_all_zero:\n    neural_tensor = neural_tensor[nonzero_trial_mask]\n```\n\niii. The observation-interval check ensures valid neural data exists for the full trial window. The reference code in the papers uses stricter trial filtering (excluding early lick, auto water, free water, ignore, and stimulation trials via `get_regular_trial_mask`), but those filters are analysis-specific; the AI correctly preserves all trial types for the decoder task. The AI documents that this drops ~22% of raw trials (94,370 to 73,910).\n\n## 2-a. What variables in the raw data is the `neural` data derived from?\n\ni. Neural data is derived from `units/spike_times` (ragged array of absolute spike times for each unit), filtered to units where `units/classification == \"good\"`.\n\nii.\n```python\nclassification = decode_strings(f[\"units\"][\"classification\"])\ngood_unit_mask = classification == \"good\"\n# ...\nspike_times_flat = f[\"units\"][\"spike_times\"][:]\nspike_times_index = f[\"units\"][\"spike_times_index\"][:]\nspike_times_ragged = split_ragged(spike_times_flat, spike_times_index)\ngood_unit_indices = np.flatnonzero(good_unit_mask)\ngood_spike_times = [np.asarray(spike_times_ragged[i], dtype=np.float64) for i in good_unit_indices]\n```\n\niii. The AI uses `classification == \"good\"` as the NWB-embedded equivalent of the external classifier-based QC lists described in the white paper. This yields 69,453 good units across 173 sessions, close to the paper's 69,943 (0.7% discrepancy attributed to archive version differences).\n\n## 2-b. How is the `neural` data processed?\n\ni. Spike times are binned into 50 ms non-overlapping bins aligned to the go cue, spanning [-2.5, +1.5] s (80 bins total). Spike counts per bin are divided by bin width (0.05 s) to produce firing rates in Hz. The result is stored as float16 per trial.\n\nii.\n```python\nBIN_WIDTH_S = 0.05\nBIN_EDGES_REL = np.arange(WINDOW_START_S, WINDOW_END_S + 1e-9, BIN_WIDTH_S, dtype=np.float64)\n# ...\nneural_tensor = np.empty((n_trials, n_good_units, N_BINS), dtype=np.float16)\nfor unit_idx, spikes in enumerate(good_spike_times):\n    edge_idx = np.searchsorted(spikes, trial_edge_matrix, side=\"left\")\n    counts = np.diff(edge_idx, axis=1)\n    neural_tensor[:, unit_idx, :] = (counts / BIN_WIDTH_S).astype(np.float16)\n```\n\niii. The reference code uses 40 ms bins with 3.4 ms stride, but the task instructions explicitly require 50 ms bins. The AI intentionally deviates from the reference bin width to match the decoder task specification. The binning is vectorized across all trials per unit using `np.searchsorted` for efficiency.\n\n## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. Only units with `classification == \"good\"` are included. No additional neural quality filtering (e.g., firing rate thresholds, ISI violation thresholds) is applied beyond the classifier label. The AI also drops trials where the resulting neural matrix is all zeros.\n\nii.\n```python\ngood_unit_mask = classification == \"good\"\nn_good_units = int(good_unit_mask.sum())\nif n_good_units == 0:\n    return None\n```\n\niii. The classifier-based QC incorporates 15 quality metrics (ISI violation, amplitude cutoff, presence ratio, drift metric, etc.) via logistic regression trained per brain region. The AI trusts this composite label rather than reimplementing individual metric thresholds. The reference code (`helper_get_neuron_id_area`) also additionally filters by hemisphere and histology-derived CCF annotations; the AI does not replicate this additional spatial filtering.\n\n## 2-d. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n\ni. Neural data is aligned to the go cue onset. For each trial, the go cue time is identified as the last `go_start_times` event within the trial's `[start_time, stop_time]` interval. Bin edges are computed relative to the go cue time: `bin_edges = go_time + BIN_EDGES_REL`.\n\nii.\n```python\ngo_candidates = interval_values(go_times, start, stop)\ngo_time = float(go_candidates[-1])\n# ...\ntrial_edge_matrix[keep_idx] = rec[\"go_time\"] + BIN_EDGES_REL\n```\n\niii. Go-cue alignment is consistent with the reference code and papers, which use \"time to go\" as the temporal reference. The use of the last go cue within each trial handles early-lick replay trials correctly.\n\n## 2-e. What is the temporal resolution (time bin size) of the converted data? Is any temporal rebinning applied?\n\ni. The time bin size is 50 ms (0.05 s), producing 80 non-overlapping bins for the [-2.5, +1.5] s window. No overlapping stride or temporal rebinning is applied; spikes are directly binned from raw spike times into the 50 ms bins.\n\nii.\n```python\nBIN_WIDTH_S = 0.05\nWINDOW_START_S = -2.5\nWINDOW_END_S = 1.5\nBIN_EDGES_REL = np.arange(WINDOW_START_S, WINDOW_END_S + 1e-9, BIN_WIDTH_S, dtype=np.float64)\nN_BINS = len(BIN_CENTERS_REL)  # 80\n```\n\niii. The task instructions specify \"50-ms-width bins for computing firing rates.\" The reference code uses 40 ms bins with 3.4 ms stride, but the AI correctly follows the task-specified 50 ms bin width. No rebinning from a finer resolution is needed since the AI bins directly from spike times.\n\n## 3-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Time from tone onset in seconds* derived from?\n\ni. This input is derived from `acquisition/BehavioralEvents/sample_start_times/timestamps` (the tone/sample onset event times) and the go cue time for temporal reference.\n\nii.\n```python\nsample_start_times = events[\"sample_start_times\"][\"timestamps\"][:].astype(np.float64)\n# ...\nsample_candidates = interval_values(sample_start_times, start, go_time)\nif len(sample_candidates) == 0:\n    sample_onset = go_time - 1.85\n    n_missing_sample_onset_fallback += 1\nelse:\n    sample_onset = float(sample_candidates[-1])\n```\n\niii. The AI uses the last `sample_start_times` event before the go cue within each trial, which handles early-lick replay trials where multiple sample events may occur. The fallback of `go_time - 1.85` is provided for trials with missing sample onset events, though in practice this fallback was never triggered (documented as 0 fallbacks across the full dataset).\n\n## 3-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Time from tone onset in seconds*?\n\ni. For each 50 ms bin, the time from tone onset is computed as: `bin_center_absolute_time - sample_onset_time`. This produces a continuous, time-varying input that increases linearly within each trial.\n\nii.\n```python\nbin_centers_abs = go_time + BIN_CENTERS_REL\ntime_from_tone = (bin_centers_abs - sample_onset).astype(np.float32)\n```\n\niii. This produces a ramp-like signal starting at a negative value (before tone onset) and increasing through the trial. The result is stored as float32.\n\n## 3-c. How is the `input` *Time from tone onset in seconds* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. The time-from-tone values are computed at the same go-cue-aligned bin centers as the neural data, ensuring perfect temporal alignment. Each bin center's value is `(go_time + bin_center_relative) - sample_onset_time`.\n\nii.\n```python\nbin_centers_abs = go_time + BIN_CENTERS_REL\ntime_from_tone = (bin_centers_abs - sample_onset).astype(np.float32)\ninput_tensor[keep_idx, 0, :] = rec[\"time_from_tone\"]\n```\n\niii. Since both neural and input data use the same bin centers (go_time + BIN_CENTERS_REL), temporal alignment is guaranteed by construction.\n\n## 4-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Photostimulation* derived from?\n\ni. Photostimulation is derived from three trial-table columns: `trials/photostim_onset` (onset time relative to trial start), `trials/photostim_duration`, and `trials/photostim_power`. All three are read as strings and parsed to floats.\n\nii.\n```python\ntrial_photostim_onset = decode_strings(trials[\"photostim_onset\"])\ntrial_photostim_duration = decode_strings(trials[\"photostim_duration\"])\ntrial_photostim_power = decode_strings(trials[\"photostim_power\"])\n```\n\niii. The AI reads these fields as strings and converts to floats, handling `N/A` values gracefully. Trials with `N/A` power have no stimulation.\n\n## 4-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Photostimulation*?\n\ni. For trials with valid photostim parameters (non-null power, onset, and duration), the AI converts the trial-relative onset to absolute time (`trial_start + onset`), computes the stimulation window, and creates a binary array where bin centers falling within the stim window are set to 1.0, all others to 0.0.\n\nii.\n```python\nphotostim_row = np.zeros(N_BINS, dtype=np.float32)\nstim_onset = parse_optional_float(trial_photostim_onset[trial_idx])\nstim_dur = parse_optional_float(trial_photostim_duration[trial_idx])\nstim_power = parse_optional_float(trial_photostim_power[trial_idx])\nif stim_power is not None and stim_onset is not None and stim_dur is not None:\n    stim_start_abs = start + stim_onset\n    stim_stop_abs = stim_start_abs + stim_dur\n    photostim_row = (\n        (bin_centers_abs >= stim_start_abs) & (bin_centers_abs < stim_stop_abs)\n    ).astype(np.float32)\n```\n\niii. The binary representation matches the task instruction \"Whether photostimulation is on at every time point (discrete, time-varying).\" The reference code aligns stimulation times to go cue; the AI instead works in absolute time and evaluates at go-aligned bin centers, which is mathematically equivalent.\n\n## 4-c. How is the `input` *Photostimulation* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. The photostimulation binary array is evaluated at the same go-cue-aligned bin centers as the neural data. Bin centers within the stimulation window are set to 1, others to 0.\n\nii.\n```python\nphotostim_row = (\n    (bin_centers_abs >= stim_start_abs) & (bin_centers_abs < stim_stop_abs)\n).astype(np.float32)\n```\n\niii. Alignment is guaranteed by using the same bin center array. The reference description indicates photoinhibition occurs during the last 0.5 s of the delay epoch (ending before the go cue), so the photostim signal should appear at negative time-to-go values, consistent with the task structure.\n\n## 5-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Lick direction choice* derived from?\n\ni. The reference code derives choice from `trial_type` (instruction) and `correctness` (outcome); the AI uses the equivalent NWB fields.\n\n## 5-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Lick direction choice*?\n\ni. For hit trials, choice = instructed side (left=0, right=1). For miss trials, choice = opposite of instructed side. For ignore trials, the AI uses the earliest lick event (left or right) within the trial interval; if no licks are found, it falls back to the instructed side. Choice is per-trial and broadcast across all 80 time bins.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef infer_choice(...) -> tuple[int, str]:\n    if outcome == \"hit\":\n        return CHOICE_MAP[instruction], \"instruction+outcome\"\n    if outcome == \"miss\":\n        opposite = \"right\" if instruction == \"left\" else \"left\"\n        return CHOICE_MAP[opposite], \"instruction+outcome\"\n    left_trial = interval_values(left_lick_times, trial_start, trial_stop)\n    right_trial = interval_values(right_lick_times, trial_start, trial_stop)\n    if len(left_trial) and len(right_trial):\n        side = \"left\" if left_trial[0] <= right_trial[0] else \"right\"\n        return CHOICE_MAP[side], \"ignore:first_lick_in_trial\"\n    if len(left_trial):\n        return CHOICE_MAP[\"left\"], \"ignore:first_lick_in_trial\"\n    if len(right_trial):\n        return CHOICE_MAP[\"right\"], \"ignore:first_lick_in_trial\"\n    return CHOICE_MAP[instruction], \"ignore:instruction_fallback\"\n```\n\niii. The reference code excludes ignore trials from analysis (`get_regular_trial_mask` removes `correctness == -1`), so there is no reference for handling this case. The AI's fallback strategy is a task-driven compromise documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md. 13,258 ignore trials used the fallback, representing ~17.9% of kept trials.\n\n## 6-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Outcome* derived from?\n\ni. Outcome is derived directly from `trials/outcome`, which contains string values \"hit\", \"miss\", or \"ignore\".\n\nii.\n```python\ntrial_outcome = decode_strings(trials[\"outcome\"])\n# ...\nOUTCOME_MAP = {\"ignore\": 0, \"miss\": 1, \"hit\": 2}\noutcome_code = OUTCOME_MAP[trial_outcome[trial_idx]]\n```\n\niii. The mapping exactly matches the task specification: ignore=0, miss=1, hit=2.\n\n## 6-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Outcome*?\n\ni. A direct string-to-integer mapping with no additional processing. The outcome code is per-trial and broadcast across all 80 time bins.\n\nii.\n```python\noutcome_code = OUTCOME_MAP[trial_outcome[trial_idx]]\noutput_row[1, :] = outcome_code\n```\n\niii. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Tongue y-position*?\n\ni. The AI applies session-level tongue y preprocessing: (1) compute frame-to-frame velocity, (2) detect outliers exceeding 5-sigma velocity threshold, (3) replace low-likelihood frames (likelihood < 0.1) with session mean y, (4) interpolate velocity-outlier frames using linear interpolation from neighboring good frames.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef process_tongue_trace(tongue_xyzl, tongue_timestamps):\n    y = tongue_xyzl[:, 1].astype(np.float64, copy=True)\n    likelihood = tongue_xyzl[:, 2].astype(np.float64, copy=False)\n    # velocity outlier detection\n    velocity = np.linalg.norm(np.diff(xy, axis=0), axis=1) / dt\n    vel_threshold = vel_mean + 5.0 * vel_std\n    # low likelihood → session mean\n    processed_y[low_likelihood_mask] = session_mean_y\n    # velocity outliers → interpolation\n    processed_y[interp_mask] = np.interp(...)\n```\n\niii. How is `output` *Tongue y-position* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. Session-level 40th and 60th percentiles of the processed tongue y are computed over ALL frames in the session (including mean-filled low-likelihood frames). Values below p40 are class 0, values between p40 and p60 (inclusive) are class 1, values above p60 are class 2.\n\nii.\n```python\np40, p60 = np.percentile(processed_y, [40.0, 60.0])\ntongue_bin = np.zeros(N_BINS, dtype=np.int16)\ntongue_bin[tongue_y >= tongue_info[\"p40\"]] = 1\ntongue_bin[tongue_y > tongue_info[\"p60\"]] = 2\n```\n\niii. The percentile computation includes mean-filled frames, which results in a heavily skewed distribution: [0.088, 0.824, 0.088] for [class 0, class 1, class 2]. This middle-class dominance occurs because many frames have tongue y at the session mean (when tongue is in the mouth/occluded), causing p40 and p60 to be close together. Despite the class imbalance, the decoder achieves 0.765 balanced accuracy for this output.\n\n## 8-d. How is `output` *Tongue y-position* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. The alignment ensures tongue y values correspond to the same time points as neural bin centers.\n\n## 9. How are minor mistakes in the data, e.g. missing data, handled?\n\ni. The AI documents fallback counts and their resolution in CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 10. All fallback mechanisms are conservative and prefer maintaining data integrity over discarding trials.\n\n## 10-a. What are the most time-consuming steps of the code?\n\ni. The most time-consuming step is reading the NWB files and performing per-unit neural binning within `process_session`. How are trials filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. The AI applies three quality filters: (1) the full go-aligned neural window [-2.5, +1.5] s must fall within a good unit's observation intervals; (2) trials without a go cue event are rejected (raises an error); (3) trials with all-zero neural activity across all units and time bins are dropped. Importantly, the AI does NOT filter out early-lick trials, ignore trials, auto-water trials, free-water trials, or photostimulation trials, because these trial types contain decoder target/input variables.\n\nii.\n```python\n# Filter 1: Observation interval coverage\ncovered = np.any(\n    (window_start >= session_obs_intervals[:, 0])\n    & (window_end <= session_obs_intervals[:, 1])\n)\nif not covered:\n    n_trials_dropped_outside_obs += 1\n    continue\n\n# Filter 3: All-zero neural trials\nnonzero_trial_mask = np.any(neural_tensor != 0, axis=(1, 2))\nn_trials_dropped_all_zero = int((~nonzero_trial_mask).sum())\nif n_trials_dropped_all_zero:\n    neural_tensor = neural_tensor[nonzero_trial_mask]\n```\n\niii. The observation-interval check ensures valid neural data exists for the full trial window. The reference code in the papers uses stricter trial filtering (excluding early lick, auto water, free water, ignore, and stimulation trials via `get_regular_trial_mask`), but those filters are analysis-specific; the AI correctly preserves all trial types for the decoder task. The AI documents that this drops ~22% of raw trials (94,370 to 73,910).\n\n## 2-a. What variables in the raw data is the `neural` data derived from?\n\ni. Neural data is derived from `units/spike_times` (ragged array of absolute spike times for each unit), filtered to units where `units/classification == \"good\"`.\n\nii.\n```python\nclassification = decode_strings(f[\"units\"][\"classification\"])\ngood_unit_mask = classification == \"good\"\n# ...\nspike_times_flat = f[\"units\"][\"spike_times\"][:]\nspike_times_index = f[\"units\"][\"spike_times_index\"][:]\nspike_times_ragged = split_ragged(spike_times_flat, spike_times_index)\ngood_unit_indices = np.flatnonzero(good_unit_mask)\ngood_spike_times = [np.asarray(spike_times_ragged[i], dtype=np.float64) for i in good_unit_indices]\n```\n\niii. The AI uses `classification == \"good\"` as the NWB-embedded equivalent of the external classifier-based QC lists described in the white paper. This yields 69,453 good units across 173 sessions, close to the paper's 69,943 (0.7% discrepancy attributed to archive version differences).\n\n## 2-b. How is the `neural` data processed?\n\ni. Spike times are binned into 50 ms non-overlapping bins aligned to the go cue, spanning [-2.5, +1.5] s (80 bins total). Spike counts per bin are divided by bin width (0.05 s) to produce firing rates in Hz. The result is stored as float16 per trial.\n\nii.\n```python\nBIN_WIDTH_S = 0.05\nBIN_EDGES_REL = np.arange(WINDOW_START_S, WINDOW_END_S + 1e-9, BIN_WIDTH_S, dtype=np.float64)\n# ...\nneural_tensor = np.empty((n_trials, n_good_units, N_BINS), dtype=np.float16)\nfor unit_idx, spikes in enumerate(good_spike_times):\n    edge_idx = np.searchsorted(spikes, trial_edge_matrix, side=\"left\")\n    counts = np.diff(edge_idx, axis=1)\n    neural_tensor[:, unit_idx, :] = (counts / BIN_WIDTH_S).astype(np.float16)\n```\n\niii. The reference code uses 40 ms bins with 3.4 ms stride, but the task instructions explicitly require 50 ms bins. The AI intentionally deviates from the reference bin width to match the decoder task specification. The binning is vectorized across all trials per unit using `np.searchsorted` for efficiency.\n\n## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. Only units with `classification == \"good\"` are included. No additional neural quality filtering (e.g., firing rate thresholds, ISI violation thresholds) is applied beyond the classifier label. The AI also drops trials where the resulting neural matrix is all zeros.\n\nii.\n```python\ngood_unit_mask = classification == \"good\"\nn_good_units = int(good_unit_mask.sum())\nif n_good_units == 0:\n    return None\n```\n\niii. The classifier-based QC incorporates 15 quality metrics (ISI violation, amplitude cutoff, presence ratio, drift metric, etc.) via logistic regression trained per brain region. The AI trusts this composite label rather than reimplementing individual metric thresholds. The reference code (`helper_get_neuron_id_area`) also additionally filters by hemisphere and histology-derived CCF annotations; the AI does not replicate this additional spatial filtering.\n\n## 2-d. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n\ni. Neural data is aligned to the go cue onset. For each trial, the go cue time is identified as the last `go_start_times` event within the trial's `[start_time, stop_time]` interval. Bin edges are computed relative to the go cue time: `bin_edges = go_time + BIN_EDGES_REL`.\n\nii.\n```python\ngo_candidates = interval_values(go_times, start, stop)\ngo_time = float(go_candidates[-1])\n# ...\ntrial_edge_matrix[keep_idx] = rec[\"go_time\"] + BIN_EDGES_REL\n```\n\niii. Go-cue alignment is consistent with the reference code and papers, which use \"time to go\" as the temporal reference. The use of the last go cue within each trial handles early-lick replay trials correctly.\n\n## 2-e. What is the temporal resolution (time bin size) of the converted data? Is any temporal rebinning applied?\n\ni. The time bin size is 50 ms (0.05 s), producing 80 non-overlapping bins for the [-2.5, +1.5] s window. No overlapping stride or temporal rebinning is applied; spikes are directly binned from raw spike times into the 50 ms bins.\n\nii.\n```python\nBIN_WIDTH_S = 0.05\nWINDOW_START_S = -2.5\nWINDOW_END_S = 1.5\nBIN_EDGES_REL = np.arange(WINDOW_START_S, WINDOW_END_S + 1e-9, BIN_WIDTH_S, dtype=np.float64)\nN_BINS = len(BIN_CENTERS_REL)  # 80\n```\n\niii. The task instructions specify \"50-ms-width bins for computing firing rates.\" The reference code uses 40 ms bins with 3.4 ms stride, but the AI correctly follows the task-specified 50 ms bin width. No rebinning from a finer resolution is needed since the AI bins directly from spike times.\n\n## 3-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Time from tone onset in seconds* derived from?\n\ni. This input is derived from `acquisition/BehavioralEvents/sample_start_times/timestamps` (the tone/sample onset event times) and the go cue time for temporal reference.\n\nii.\n```python\nsample_start_times = events[\"sample_start_times\"][\"timestamps\"][:].astype(np.float64)\n# ...\nsample_candidates = interval_values(sample_start_times, start, go_time)\nif len(sample_candidates) == 0:\n    sample_onset = go_time - 1.85\n    n_missing_sample_onset_fallback += 1\nelse:\n    sample_onset = float(sample_candidates[-1])\n```\n\niii. The AI uses the last `sample_start_times` event before the go cue within each trial, which handles early-lick replay trials where multiple sample events may occur. The fallback of `go_time - 1.85` is provided for trials with missing sample onset events, though in practice this fallback was never triggered (documented as 0 fallbacks across the full dataset).\n\n## 3-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Time from tone onset in seconds*?\n\ni. For each 50 ms bin, the time from tone onset is computed as: `bin_center_absolute_time - sample_onset_time`. This produces a continuous, time-varying input that increases linearly within each trial.\n\nii.\n```python\nbin_centers_abs = go_time + BIN_CENTERS_REL\ntime_from_tone = (bin_centers_abs - sample_onset).astype(np.float32)\n```\n\niii. This produces a ramp-like signal starting at a negative value (before tone onset) and increasing through the trial. The result is stored as float32.\n\n## 3-c. How is the `input` *Time from tone onset in seconds* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. The time-from-tone values are computed at the same go-cue-aligned bin centers as the neural data, ensuring perfect temporal alignment. Each bin center's value is `(go_time + bin_center_relative) - sample_onset_time`.\n\nii.\n```python\nbin_centers_abs = go_time + BIN_CENTERS_REL\ntime_from_tone = (bin_centers_abs - sample_onset).astype(np.float32)\ninput_tensor[keep_idx, 0, :] = rec[\"time_from_tone\"]\n```\n\niii. Since both neural and input data use the same bin centers (go_time + BIN_CENTERS_REL), temporal alignment is guaranteed by construction.\n\n## 4-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Photostimulation* derived from?\n\ni. Photostimulation is derived from three trial-table columns: `trials/photostim_onset` (onset time relative to trial start), `trials/photostim_duration`, and `trials/photostim_power`. All three are read as strings and parsed to floats.\n\nii.\n```python\ntrial_photostim_onset = decode_strings(trials[\"photostim_onset\"])\ntrial_photostim_duration = decode_strings(trials[\"photostim_duration\"])\ntrial_photostim_power = decode_strings(trials[\"photostim_power\"])\n```\n\niii. The AI reads these fields as strings and converts to floats, handling `N/A` values gracefully. Trials with `N/A` power have no stimulation.\n\n## 4-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Photostimulation*?\n\ni. For trials with valid photostim parameters (non-null power, onset, and duration), the AI converts the trial-relative onset to absolute time (`trial_start + onset`), computes the stimulation window, and creates a binary array where bin centers falling within the stim window are set to 1.0, all others to 0.0.\n\nii.\n```python\nphotostim_row = np.zeros(N_BINS, dtype=np.float32)\nstim_onset = parse_optional_float(trial_photostim_onset[trial_idx])\nstim_dur = parse_optional_float(trial_photostim_duration[trial_idx])\nstim_power = parse_optional_float(trial_photostim_power[trial_idx])\nif stim_power is not None and stim_onset is not None and stim_dur is not None:\n    stim_start_abs = start + stim_onset\n    stim_stop_abs = stim_start_abs + stim_dur\n    photostim_row = (\n        (bin_centers_abs >= stim_start_abs) & (bin_centers_abs < stim_stop_abs)\n    ).astype(np.float32)\n```\n\niii. The binary representation matches the task instruction \"Whether photostimulation is on at every time point (discrete, time-varying).\" The reference code aligns stimulation times to go cue; the AI instead works in absolute time and evaluates at go-aligned bin centers, which is mathematically equivalent.\n\n## 4-c. How is the `input` *Photostimulation* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. The photostimulation binary array is evaluated at the same go-cue-aligned bin centers as the neural data. Bin centers within the stimulation window are set to 1, others to 0.\n\nii.\n```python\nphotostim_row = (\n    (bin_centers_abs >= stim_start_abs) & (bin_centers_abs < stim_stop_abs)\n).astype(np.float32)\n```\n\niii. Alignment is guaranteed by using the same bin center array. The reference description indicates photoinhibition occurs during the last 0.5 s of the delay epoch (ending before the go cue), so the photostim signal should appear at negative time-to-go values, consistent with the task structure.\n\n## 5-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Lick direction choice* derived from?\n\ni. Choice is inferred from `trials/trial_instruction` (left/right), `trials/outcome` (hit/miss/ignore), and for ignore trials, from `acquisition/BehavioralEvents/left_lick_times` and `right_lick_times`.\n\nii.\n```python\ntrial_instruction = decode_strings(trials[\"trial_instruction\"])\ntrial_outcome = decode_strings(trials[\"outcome\"])\n# ...\nleft_lick_times = events[\"left_lick_times\"][\"timestamps\"][:].astype(np.float64)\nright_lick_times = events[\"right_lick_times\"][\"timestamps\"][:].astype(np.float64)\n```\n\niii. The reference code derives choice from `trial_type` (instruction) and `correctness` (outcome); the AI uses the equivalent NWB fields.\n\n## 5-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Lick direction choice*?\n\ni. For hit trials, choice = instructed side (left=0, right=1). For miss trials, choice = opposite of instructed side. For ignore trials, the AI uses the earliest lick event (left or right) within the trial interval; if no licks are found, it falls back to the instructed side. Choice is per-trial and broadcast across all 80 time bins.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef infer_choice(...) -> tuple[int, str]:\n    if outcome == \"hit\":\n        return CHOICE_MAP[instruction], \"instruction+outcome\"\n    if outcome == \"miss\":\n        opposite = \"right\" if instruction == \"left\" else \"left\"\n        return CHOICE_MAP[opposite], \"instruction+outcome\"\n    left_trial = interval_values(left_lick_times, trial_start, trial_stop)\n    right_trial = interval_values(right_lick_times, trial_start, trial_stop)\n    if len(left_trial) and len(right_trial):\n        side = \"left\" if left_trial[0] <= right_trial[0] else \"right\"\n        return CHOICE_MAP[side], \"ignore:first_lick_in_trial\"\n    if len(left_trial):\n        return CHOICE_MAP[\"left\"], \"ignore:first_lick_in_trial\"\n    if len(right_trial):\n        return CHOICE_MAP[\"right\"], \"ignore:first_lick_in_trial\"\n    return CHOICE_MAP[instruction], \"ignore:instruction_fallback\"\n```\n\niii. The reference code excludes ignore trials from analysis (`get_regular_trial_mask` removes `correctness == -1`), so there is no reference for handling this case. The AI's fallback strategy is a task-driven compromise documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md. 13,258 ignore trials used the fallback, representing ~17.9% of kept trials.\n\n## 6-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Outcome* derived from?\n\ni. Outcome is derived directly from `trials/outcome`, which contains string values \"hit\", \"miss\", or \"ignore\".\n\nii.\n```python\ntrial_outcome = decode_strings(trials[\"outcome\"])\n# ...\nOUTCOME_MAP = {\"ignore\": 0, \"miss\": 1, \"hit\": 2}\noutcome_code = OUTCOME_MAP[trial_outcome[trial_idx]]\n```\n\niii. The mapping exactly matches the task specification: ignore=0, miss=1, hit=2.\n\n## 6-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Outcome*?\n\ni. A direct string-to-integer mapping with no additional processing. The outcome code is per-trial and broadcast across all 80 time bins.\n\nii.\n```python\noutcome_code = OUTCOME_MAP[trial_outcome[trial_idx]]\noutput_row[1, :] = outcome_code\n```\n\niii. No transformation beyond the specified mapping is needed.\n\n## 6-c. How is `output` *Outcome* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Outcome is a per-trial scalar replicated across all time bins, so no temporal alignment is required beyond the trial-level correspondence.\n\nii.\n```python\noutput_row = np.empty((4, N_BINS), dtype=np.int16)\noutput_row[1, :] = outcome_code\n```\n\niii. The task specification states outcome is \"per-trial\", so broadcasting it across all time bins is the correct approach for the time-varying output format.\n\n## 7-a. Tongue y-position is derived from `acquisition/BehavioralTimeSeries/Camera0_side_TongueTracking`, which contains per-frame data with columns (x, y, likelihood) and associated timestamps at ~300 Hz.\n\nii.\n```python\ntongue_group = f[\"acquisition\"][\"BehavioralTimeSeries\"][\"Camera0_side_TongueTracking\"]\ntongue_data = tongue_group[\"data\"][:].astype(np.float64)\ntongue_timestamps = tongue_group[\"timestamps\"][:].astype(np.float64)\n```\n\niii. The AI uses the side-view tongue tracking data, consistent with the method paper's description of side-view camera tracking of tongue, jaw, and nose movements.\n\n## 8-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Tongue y-position*?\n\ni. The AI applies session-level tongue y preprocessing: (1) compute frame-to-frame velocity, (2) detect outliers exceeding 5-sigma velocity threshold, (3) replace low-likelihood frames (likelihood < 0.1) with session mean y, (4) interpolate velocity-outlier frames using linear interpolation from neighboring good frames.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef process_tongue_trace(tongue_xyzl, tongue_timestamps):\n    y = tongue_xyzl[:, 1].astype(np.float64, copy=True)\n    likelihood = tongue_xyzl[:, 2].astype(np.float64, copy=False)\n    # velocity outlier detection\n    velocity = np.linalg.norm(np.diff(xy, axis=0), axis=1) / dt\n    vel_threshold = vel_mean + 5.0 * vel_std\n    # low likelihood → session mean\n    processed_y[low_likelihood_mask] = session_mean_y\n    # velocity outliers → interpolation\n    processed_y[interp_mask] = np.interp(...)\n```\n\niii. This matches the method paper's description: \"marker outliers removed using a five-sigma velocity threshold and imputed from nearby frames\" and \"when the tongue is occluded in the mouth, tongue position is set to its mean value.\" The likelihood threshold of 0.1 is a reasonable choice for DeepLabCut tracking confidence.\n\n## 8-c. How is `output` *Tongue y-position* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. Session-level 40th and 60th percentiles of the processed tongue y are computed over ALL frames in the session (including mean-filled low-likelihood frames). Values below p40 are class 0, values between p40 and p60 (inclusive) are class 1, values above p60 are class 2.\n\nii.\n```python\np40, p60 = np.percentile(processed_y, [40.0, 60.0])\ntongue_bin = np.zeros(N_BINS, dtype=np.int16)\ntongue_bin[tongue_y >= tongue_info[\"p40\"]] = 1\ntongue_bin[tongue_y > tongue_info[\"p60\"]] = 2\n```\n\niii. The percentile computation includes mean-filled frames, which results in a heavily skewed distribution: [0.088, 0.824, 0.088] for [class 0, class 1, class 2]. This middle-class dominance occurs because many frames have tongue y at the session mean (when tongue is in the mouth/occluded), causing p40 and p60 to be close together. Despite the class imbalance, the decoder achieves 0.765 balanced accuracy for this output.\n\n## 8-d. How is `output` *Tongue y-position* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. For each go-aligned bin center, the AI finds the closest preceding tongue tracking frame using `np.searchsorted(..., side=\"right\") - 1` and reads the processed tongue y value at that frame index.\n\nii.\n```python\ntongue_frame_idx = np.searchsorted(tongue_timestamps, bin_centers_abs, side=\"right\") - 1\ntongue_frame_idx = np.clip(tongue_frame_idx, 0, len(processed_tongue_y) - 1)\ntongue_y = processed_tongue_y[tongue_frame_idx]\n```\n\niii. This nearest-preceding-frame approach is similar to the reference code's `align_markers_between_lims`, which selects the last frame within each time bin window. The alignment ensures tongue y values correspond to the same time points as neural bin centers.\n\n## 9. How are minor mistakes in the data, e.g. missing data, handled?\n\ni. The AI handles several missing/edge-case data scenarios:\n- **Missing sample onset**: If no `sample_start_times` event is found before the go cue, falls back to `go_time - 1.85 s` (0 occurrences in practice).\n- **Missing choice on ignore trials**: Uses first lick side in trial; if no licks, uses instructed side as placeholder.\n- **Low-likelihood tongue frames**: Replaced with session mean tongue y.\n- **Velocity-outlier tongue frames**: Linearly interpolated from neighboring good frames.\n- **Byte-string encoding artifacts in subject IDs**: Stripped `b'...'` prefix.\n- **Photostim fields stored as strings including \"N/A\"**: Parsed with `parse_optional_float` that returns None for N/A values.\n- **Trials outside observation intervals**: Dropped to avoid invalid neural data.\n- **All-zero neural trials**: Dropped as a final safety check.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef parse_optional_float(value: str) -> float | None:\n    if value in {\"N/A\", \"\", \"nan\", \"None\"}:\n        return None\n    return float(value)\n```\n\niii. The AI documents fallback counts and their resolution in CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 10. All fallback mechanisms are conservative and prefer maintaining data integrity over discarding trials.\n\n## 10-a. What are the most time-consuming steps of the code?\n\ni. The most time-consuming step is reading the NWB files and performing per-unit neural binning within `process_session`. Loading spike times (ragged arrays) from HDF5 and the vectorized `searchsorted` binning across all trials dominate per-session time. The AI reports ~0.70 s per session on average, with full conversion taking ~2.35 minutes for 173 sessions.\n\nii.\n```python\nspike_times_flat = f[\"units\"][\"spike_times\"][:]\nspike_times_index = f[\"units\"][\"spike_times_index\"][:]\n# ...\nfor unit_idx, spikes in enumerate(good_spike_times):\n    edge_idx = np.searchsorted(spikes, trial_edge_matrix, side=\"left\")\n    counts = np.diff(edge_idx, axis=1)\n    neural_tensor[:, unit_idx, :] = (counts / BIN_WIDTH_S).astype(np.float16)\n```\n\niii. The per-trial loop over trial records (lines 248-319) iterates through each trial to compute go cue, sample onset, photostim, choice, tongue y, and outputs individually. While some operations within the loop are vectorized, the overall trial-level iteration could potentially be restructured to batch-process multiple trials at once. The per-unit loop for neural binning (line 339) iterates over good units but this is already semi-vectorized (each unit processes all trials at once).\n\nii.\n```python\nfor trial_idx in range(len(trial_start)):\n    # ... per-trial processing ...\n```\n\niii.
